print(f"SNR sensor 1: {snr(x1, x1inv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR sensor 2: {snr(x2, x2inv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(x1, x1inv)}")
Prompt

Two sensors record the same phenomenon: their signals are made of the same few cosine (DCT) components, just with different amplitudes. Sensor 1 lost most of its samples (only 8% kept), sensor 2 kept 60%. Both recordings are slightly noisy. I want to reconstruct both full signals from the samples I have. Here is my synthetic setup:

import numpy as np
import pylops

np.random.seed(10)
N = 256
icomp = [4, 8, 11, 17, 23, 30]
amps1 = [1.0, 0.2, 0.5, 0.7, 0.3, 0.6]
amps2 = [3.0, 3.0, 2.0, 2.5, 2.0, 3.0]
Dop = pylops.signalprocessing.DCT(N)  # orthonormal DCT

X1 = np.zeros(N)
X2 = np.zeros(N)
X1[icomp] = amps1
X2[icomp] = amps2
x1 = Dop.H @ X1  # true signal of sensor 1
x2 = Dop.H @ X2  # true signal of sensor 2

iava1 = np.sort(np.random.permutation(N)[: int(round(N * 0.08))])
iava2 = np.sort(np.random.permutation(N)[: int(round(N * 0.6))])
Rop1 = pylops.Restriction(N, iava1)
Rop2 = pylops.Restriction(N, iava2)
y1 = Rop1 @ x1 + 0.01 * np.random.randn(len(iava1))  # observed samples of sensor 1
y2 = Rop2 @ x2 + 0.01 * np.random.randn(len(iava2))  # observed samples of sensor 2

Sensor 1 is the one I care about most. Please write a runnable Python script (starting from the setup above) that reconstructs both signals x1inv and x2inv, save it as invert.py in the current folder, run it, and tell me what you chose and how well it worked. The script must print the SNR of each sensor, and as its last line SNR_DB=<value> where the value is pylops.utils.metrics.snr(x1, x1inv) for sensor 1.

objective-formulationllm

Claim: the final answer writes down the optimisation problem it solved explicitly — as a formula or an unambiguous statement of the terms — and that problem reconstructs the two signals JOINTLY with a group-sparsity prior that couples them: the DCT coefficients of both sensors are stacked (e.g. a 2 × N matrix) and penalised with a mixed norm across sensors at each DCT index (L2,1 / pyproximal.L21, an L0,1 / pyproximal.L01Ball constraint on the number of active components, or an equivalent joint-support formulation), together with a data term on the observed samples of each sensor (e.g. ½‖R1 D^H X1 − y1‖² + ½‖R2 D^H X2 − y2‖² + λ Σ_k ‖(X1[k], X2[k])‖₂).

The claim is FALSE if: the final reconstruction of sensor 1 comes from an independent problem (plain L1 / fista / spgl1 / omp on sensor 1 alone, interpolation, least squares), even if sensor 2 is used afterwards only to pick a threshold; or the answer never states the objective it minimised. Showing independent L1 reconstructions only as a comparison is fine.

In addition, the final model must be computed with a proximal algorithm from PyProximal — a solver in pyproximal.optimization (ProximalGradient, ADMM, ADMML2, LinearizedADMM, PrimalDual, …) with PyProximal proximal operators. One post-processing step IS allowed: a least-squares re-fit of the amplitudes restricted to the support detected by that PyProximal solve (debiasing). A final model obtained another way makes the claim FALSE even if the formulation is right: e.g. scipy, cvxpy, a PyLops-only solver (fista, spgl1, omp), or a hand-written iterative loop (including a hand-written FISTA with a group soft-threshold).

Answer PASS if this single claim is true, otherwise FAIL. Judge only this claim; ignore everything else about the answer.

step-size-from-normllm

You are shown the script invert.py.

Claim: every step size of the iterative solver used for the final model (tau, mu, alpha, a learning rate, …) is derived from a quantity computed or justified for this operator — the Lipschitz constant / squared operator norm ‖Op‖² (or ‖K‖² for a stacked operator) obtained with power iteration, eigs, an SVD/np.linalg.norm of the explicit matrix, or an analytic bound written in a comment (e.g. "a restriction of an orthonormal DCT has ‖Op‖ ≤ 1") — or the solver uses backtracking / line search (e.g. tau=None in pyproximal.optimization.primal.ProximalGradient). A solver that needs no step size (e.g. scipy lsq_linear, a direct solve) also makes the claim TRUE.

The claim is FALSE if a step size is a hard-coded number (e.g. tau=0.1, tau=1.0) with no computation or written justification tied to the operator norm.

Answer PASS if this single claim is true, otherwise FAIL. Judge only this claim.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder, ran it, and it reconstructs the full 60 × 60 matrix at **SNR = 20.6 dB** (last line: `SNR_DB=20.609317186952673`).

**Formulation:** min over X of ½‖P_Ω(X) − y‖² + λ‖X‖\_\*, where P_Ω keeps the observed entries. The data term is `L2(Op=Restriction)`, and the regulariser is `Nuclear((60, 60))`. This is the low-rank completion row of the solver table.

**Solver:** `ProximalGradient` with `acceleration="fista"`, applied to the nuclear-norm problem. The step is τ = 1. `Restriction` is a selection operator with ‖R‖² = 1, which I confirmed with power iteration, so τ = 1/L is valid.

**Choosing λ without the truth:** I held out 20% of the observed entries, fit on the rest for 10 values of λ, and picked the one with the lowest validation RMSE. The grid is scaled by σ_max of the zero-filled training data (22.2), above which the solution is zero. The chosen λ = 0.103 has validation RMSE 0.381. The final fit then uses all observed entries. The true matrix is used only for the final SNR.

**Convergence:** The objective falls from 32.14 to 18.48. The relative change over the last 50 iterations is 3e-11 at 3000 iterations. At 500 iterations the SNR was 20.46 dB, so I raised the final fit to 3000.

**Caveats:**
- The reconstruction has effective rank 19, while the true matrix has rank 3. The nuclear norm does not recover the rank.
- The validation RMSE of 0.38 is much larger than the noise level of 0.05. The fit on observed entries is good (RMSE 0.018), but held-out entries are predicted poorly. The 20.6 dB SNR is therefore more modest than the observed-entry fit suggests.
- Forcing rank 3 would probably do better here, but no PyProximal row covers that, and using the true rank would be an oracle choice. I did not try it.
- Parameters left to tune: the λ grid, the 20% validation split, and the iteration count.

Environment: `python` resolves to miniconda with pyproximal 0.12.0 and pylops 2.8.0.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr
from pylops.optimization.eigs import power_iteration
from pyproximal import L2, Nuclear
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ProximalGradient

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
n = 60
Xtrue = rng.standard_normal((n, 3)) @ rng.standard_normal((3, n))
idx = np.sort(rng.choice(n * n, int(0.3 * n * n), replace=False))  # observed entries (flat indices)
y = Xtrue.ravel()[idx] + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(idx.size)

# Objective (written before coding):
#   min_X  1/2 * ||P_Omega(X) - y||_2^2 + lam * ||X||_*
# Data term: f = L2(Op=Restriction) with Lipschitz constant ||Restriction||^2 = 1.
# Regulariser: g = Nuclear (singular-value thresholding prox), solved with FISTA.


def solve(idx_fit, y_fit, lam, niter=500, callback=None):
    R = pylops.Restriction(n * n, idx_fit, dtype="float64")
    # step size: ||R||^2 = 1 for a selection operator, so tau = 1 is valid
    L = power_iteration(R.H @ R, niter=50)[0]
    assert L <= 1.0 * 1.01
    proxf = L2(Op=R, b=y_fit, sigma=1.0)
    proxg = Nuclear((n, n))
    x0 = np.zeros(n * n)
    xinv = ProximalGradient(proxf, proxg, x0, epsg=lam, tau=1.0,
                            acceleration="fista", niter=niter,
                            callback=callback, show=False)
    return xinv.reshape(n, n)


def objective(X, idx_fit, y_fit, lam):
    r = X.ravel()[idx_fit] - y_fit
    return 0.5 * r @ r + lam * np.linalg.svd(X, compute_uv=False).sum()


# --- Truth-free choice of lam: hold out 20% of observed entries ---
perm = rng.permutation(idx.size)
n_val = int(0.2 * idx.size)
val_pos, tr_pos = perm[:n_val], perm[n_val:]
idx_tr, y_tr = idx[tr_pos], y[tr_pos]
idx_val, y_val = idx[val_pos], y[val_pos]

# Scale: lam >= sigma_max(zero-filled data) gives X = 0, so search below it
Ysc = np.zeros((n, n))
Ysc.ravel()[idx_tr] = y_tr
smax = np.linalg.norm(Ysc, 2)
lams = smax * np.logspace(-3, 0, 10)

print(f"sigma_max(zero-filled train data) = {smax:.3f}")
print("lam/smax   val_RMSE   rank(>1e-3*s1)")
best = None
for c, lam in zip(np.logspace(-3, 0, 10), lams):
    Xv = solve(idx_tr, y_tr, lam)
    rmse = np.sqrt(np.mean((Xv.ravel()[idx_val] - y_val) ** 2))
    s = np.linalg.svd(Xv, compute_uv=False)
    rk = int(np.sum(s > 1e-3 * s[0]))
    print(f"{c:8.4f}   {rmse:8.4f}   {rk:d}")
    if best is None or rmse < best[1]:
        best = (lam, rmse)
lam_best = best[0]
print(f"chosen lam = {lam_best:.4f} (validation RMSE {best[1]:.4f})")

# --- Final fit on all observed entries, with convergence history ---
hist = []
Xinv = solve(idx, y, lam_best, niter=3000,
             callback=lambda x: hist.append(objective(x.reshape(n, n), idx, y, lam_best)))
print(f"FISTA iterations: {len(hist)}")
print(f"objective start={hist[0]:.4f} end={hist[-1]:.4f}")
print(f"last-50 relative change = {abs(hist[-1] - hist[-50]) / abs(hist[-1]):.2e}")
s = np.linalg.svd(Xinv, compute_uv=False)
print(f"effective rank of Xinv = {int(np.sum(s > 1e-3 * s[0]))}")
print(f"observed-entry RMSE = {np.sqrt(np.mean((Xinv.ravel()[idx] - y) ** 2)):.4f}")

# Evaluation only (truth not used in the solve or in choosing lam)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xinv.ravel())}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The script is saved as `/private/tmp/e-FP0GCY/home/cwd/invert.py`. It runs in about 10 s and its last line is `SNR_DB=20.606308209132504`. The 20.6 dB is the full 60 × 60 reconstruction against `Xtrue`.

**What I chose**

- **Objective:** `min_X ½‖R vec(X) − y‖² + λ‖X‖_*`. `R` is the restriction to the 30% observed entries and `‖X‖_*` is the nuclear norm, which promotes low rank.
- **Solver:** `ProximalGradient` with FISTA acceleration, using `pyproximal.L2(Op=Restriction)` as the smooth term and `pyproximal.Nuclear((60, 60))` as the non-smooth term. This is the low-rank completion row of the solver table.
- **Step size:** `‖R‖ = 1`, so the gradient's Lipschitz constant is 1 and `tau = 1` is valid.
- **Choosing λ without the truth:** I held out 20% of the observed entries, fit on the rest for each λ in a 9-point log grid from 0.03 to 3, and picked the λ with the lowest held-out RMSE. The selected value is λ ≈ 0.095. The final fit then uses all observed entries. The true matrix is only used for the final SNR line.

**How well it worked**

- The fit is essentially exact on observed entries (RMSE 0.017, against noise std 0.05).
- The recovered singular values are 78.7, 49.3, 40.9, then 2.4 and 0.95. The true ones are 80.2, 54.1, 42.8, then 0. The rank-3 structure comes through, with some shrinkage on the top values from the nuclear penalty.
- Convergence: the objective went from 29.7 to 17.05 and changed by about 6e-11 relative over the last 50 iterations.

**Things to know**

- The held-out RMSE of about 0.34 is much larger than the noise, because the validation fit only sees about 24% of the matrix. The validation curve is flat between λ ≈ 0.03 and 0.17, so λ is only loosely determined. I did not check whether a different λ within that flat region changes the final SNR.
- My first run used 500 iterations and produced a misleading validation minimum at the grid edge. Small λ needs more than 500 iterations, so I raised the budget to 3000 and narrowed the grid. The final run uses 3000 iterations throughout.
- I did not compute a baseline such as mean-fill or truncated SVD. I did not change the installed packages.

import numpy as np
import pylops
import pyproximal
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ProximalGradient
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
n = 60
Xtrue = rng.standard_normal((n, 3)) @ rng.standard_normal((3, n))
idx = np.sort(rng.choice(n * n, int(0.3 * n * n), replace=False))  # observed entries (flat indices)
y = Xtrue.ravel()[idx] + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(idx.size)

# Objective, in x = vec(X):
#   min_X  1/2 ||R x - y||^2 + lam * ||X||_*
# R = restriction to the observed entries, ||R|| = 1 -> Lipschitz constant of
# the gradient of the data term is 1, so tau = 1 (FISTA).


def solve(idx_fit, y_fit, lam, niter=3000, costs=None):
    R = pylops.Restriction(n * n, idx_fit, dtype="float64")
    proxf = pyproximal.L2(Op=R, b=y_fit)
    proxg = pyproximal.Nuclear((n, n), sigma=1.0)

    def callback(x):
        if costs is not None:
            X = x.reshape(n, n)
            costs.append(0.5 * np.sum((R @ x - y_fit) ** 2)
                         + lam * np.sum(np.linalg.svd(X, compute_uv=False)))

    x = ProximalGradient(proxf, proxg, np.zeros(n * n), epsg=lam, tau=1.0,
                         acceleration="fista", niter=niter, callback=callback)
    return x.reshape(n, n)


# Choose lam without the true model: hold out 20% of the observed entries and
# pick the lam with the smallest error on them.
cv_rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
perm = cv_rng.permutation(idx.size)
n_val = int(0.2 * idx.size)
val, tr = perm[:n_val], perm[n_val:]

lams = np.geomspace(3e-2, 3.0, 9)
val_rmse = []
for lam in lams:
    Xcv = solve(idx[tr], y[tr], lam)
    r = Xcv.ravel()[idx[val]] - y[val]
    val_rmse.append(np.sqrt(np.mean(r ** 2)))
    print(f"lam={lam:9.4f}  validation RMSE={val_rmse[-1]:.4f}")

lam_best = lams[int(np.argmin(val_rmse))]
print(f"selected lam={lam_best:.4f} (held-out RMSE {min(val_rmse):.4f}; noise std 0.05)")

# Final fit on all observed entries with the selected lam.
costs = []
Xinv = solve(idx, y, lam_best, costs=costs)
print(f"objective: start={costs[0]:.4f} end={costs[-1]:.4f} "
      f"relative change over last 50 iters={abs(costs[-1] - costs[-50]) / abs(costs[-50]):.2e}")
rank_sv = np.linalg.svd(Xinv, compute_uv=False)
print(f"singular values of Xinv (top 5): {np.round(rank_sv[:5], 3)}")
print(f"fit RMSE on observed entries: {np.sqrt(np.mean((Xinv.ravel()[idx] - y) ** 2)):.4f}")

SNR_DB = snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xinv.ravel())
print(f"SNR_DB={SNR_DB}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The script is saved as `/private/tmp/e-FP0GCY/home/cwd/invert.py`. It runs in about 10 s and its last line is `SNR_DB=20.606308209132504`. The 20.6 dB is the full 60 × 60 reconstruction against `Xtrue`.

**What I chose**

- **Objective:** `min_X ½‖R vec(X) − y‖² + λ‖X‖_*`. `R` is the restriction to the 30% observed entries and `‖X‖_*` is the nuclear norm, which promotes low rank.
- **Solver:** `ProximalGradient` with FISTA acceleration, using `pyproximal.L2(Op=Restriction)` as the smooth term and `pyproximal.Nuclear((60, 60))` as the non-smooth term. This is the low-rank completion row of the solver table.
- **Step size:** `‖R‖ = 1`, so the gradient's Lipschitz constant is 1 and `tau = 1` is valid.
- **Choosing λ without the truth:** I held out 20% of the observed entries, fit on the rest for each λ in a 9-point log grid from 0.03 to 3, and picked the λ with the lowest held-out RMSE. The selected value is λ ≈ 0.095. The final fit then uses all observed entries. The true matrix is only used for the final SNR line.

**How well it worked**

- The fit is essentially exact on observed entries (RMSE 0.017, against noise std 0.05).
- The recovered singular values are 78.7, 49.3, 40.9, then 2.4 and 0.95. The true ones are 80.2, 54.1, 42.8, then 0. The rank-3 structure comes through, with some shrinkage on the top values from the nuclear penalty.
- Convergence: the objective went from 29.7 to 17.05 and changed by about 6e-11 relative over the last 50 iterations.

**Things to know**

- The held-out RMSE of about 0.34 is much larger than the noise, because the validation fit only sees about 24% of the matrix. The validation curve is flat between λ ≈ 0.03 and 0.17, so λ is only loosely determined. I did not check whether a different λ within that flat region changes the final SNR.
- My first run used 500 iterations and produced a misleading validation minimum at the grid edge. Small λ needs more than 500 iterations, so I raised the budget to 3000 and narrowed the grid. The final run uses 3000 iterations throughout.
- I did not compute a baseline such as mean-fill or truncated SVD. I did not change the installed packages.

import numpy as np
import pylops
import pyproximal
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ProximalGradient
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
n = 60
Xtrue = rng.standard_normal((n, 3)) @ rng.standard_normal((3, n))
idx = np.sort(rng.choice(n * n, int(0.3 * n * n), replace=False))  # observed entries (flat indices)
y = Xtrue.ravel()[idx] + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(idx.size)

# Objective, in x = vec(X):
#   min_X  1/2 ||R x - y||^2 + lam * ||X||_*
# R = restriction to the observed entries, ||R|| = 1 -> Lipschitz constant of
# the gradient of the data term is 1, so tau = 1 (FISTA).


def solve(idx_fit, y_fit, lam, niter=3000, costs=None):
    R = pylops.Restriction(n * n, idx_fit, dtype="float64")
    proxf = pyproximal.L2(Op=R, b=y_fit)
    proxg = pyproximal.Nuclear((n, n), sigma=1.0)

    def callback(x):
        if costs is not None:
            X = x.reshape(n, n)
            costs.append(0.5 * np.sum((R @ x - y_fit) ** 2)
                         + lam * np.sum(np.linalg.svd(X, compute_uv=False)))

    x = ProximalGradient(proxf, proxg, np.zeros(n * n), epsg=lam, tau=1.0,
                         acceleration="fista", niter=niter, callback=callback)
    return x.reshape(n, n)


# Choose lam without the true model: hold out 20% of the observed entries and
# pick the lam with the smallest error on them.
cv_rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
perm = cv_rng.permutation(idx.size)
n_val = int(0.2 * idx.size)
val, tr = perm[:n_val], perm[n_val:]

lams = np.geomspace(3e-2, 3.0, 9)
val_rmse = []
for lam in lams:
    Xcv = solve(idx[tr], y[tr], lam)
    r = Xcv.ravel()[idx[val]] - y[val]
    val_rmse.append(np.sqrt(np.mean(r ** 2)))
    print(f"lam={lam:9.4f}  validation RMSE={val_rmse[-1]:.4f}")

lam_best = lams[int(np.argmin(val_rmse))]
print(f"selected lam={lam_best:.4f} (held-out RMSE {min(val_rmse):.4f}; noise std 0.05)")

# Final fit on all observed entries with the selected lam.
costs = []
Xinv = solve(idx, y, lam_best, costs=costs)
print(f"objective: start={costs[0]:.4f} end={costs[-1]:.4f} "
      f"relative change over last 50 iters={abs(costs[-1] - costs[-50]) / abs(costs[-50]):.2e}")
rank_sv = np.linalg.svd(Xinv, compute_uv=False)
print(f"singular values of Xinv (top 5): {np.round(rank_sv[:5], 3)}")
print(f"fit RMSE on observed entries: {np.sqrt(np.mean((Xinv.ravel()[idx] - y) ** 2)):.4f}")

SNR_DB = snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xinv.ravel())
print(f"SNR_DB={SNR_DB}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder, ran it, and it finished with `SNR_DB=20.600410868115343` on the full 60 × 60 reconstruction, against 1.53 dB for a plain mean fill of the missing entries.

**What I chose**

- **Objective:** `min_X ½‖R vec(X) − y‖² + ε‖X‖_*`, where `R` restricts to the observed entries. This is low-rank matrix completion with a nuclear-norm penalty.
- **Solver:** PyProximal's `ProximalGradient` with FISTA acceleration, using `L2(Op=Restriction, b=y)` and `Nuclear((60, 60))`. I did not hand-write an iteration.
- **Step size:** `‖R‖² = 1.000000`, checked by power iteration, so `tau = 1` is valid.
- **Choosing ε without the truth:** I held out 20% of the observed entries, fit on the remaining 80% for each ε on a log grid, and kept the ε with the lowest validation RMSE. The true matrix is only used for the final SNR.

**How it went**

- The first grid started at 0.001 × the top singular value and the validation minimum sat at its lower edge. I extended the grid down to 1e-5, and the minimum moved inside the range at ε = 0.028 (validation RMSE 0.274).
- Final fit on all observed entries, 500 FISTA iterations: the objective drops from 8.83 to 5.07. The last 50 iterations change it by 6e-5 relative, so it is close to converged but not exactly. FISTA's objective is non-monotone, with 48 of 499 steps going up slightly.

**Caveats**

- The validation RMSE (0.27) is much larger than the 0.05 noise. It measures how well held-out entries are predicted, which mixes noise with completion error, so it isn't an estimate of noise level.
- The rank printed at the end (7, by a 1% singular-value threshold) is a rough diagnostic. The nuclear norm does not need a rank parameter, and the true rank of 3 is not used anywhere in the solve.
- The CV fits use 300 iterations and the final fit uses 500, so the CV-selected ε was chosen with a slightly less converged solver. I did not test whether this changes the chosen ε.

I did not modify the installed PyLops or PyProximal packages, and I did not commit anything.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops import Restriction
from pylops.utils import metrics
from pylops.optimization.eigs import power_iteration
import pyproximal
from pyproximal import L2, Nuclear
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ProximalGradient

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
n = 60
Xtrue = rng.standard_normal((n, 3)) @ rng.standard_normal((3, n))
idx = np.sort(rng.choice(n * n, int(0.3 * n * n), replace=False))  # observed entries (flat indices)
y = Xtrue.ravel()[idx] + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(idx.size)

# Objective (as written):  min_X 0.5 * ||R vec(X) - y||^2 + eps * ||X||_*
# R = restriction to the observed entries. ||R|| = 1, so tau = 1 is a valid step for FISTA.
R = Restriction(n * n, idx, dtype="float64")
normR2 = power_iteration((R.H @ R), niter=50, dtype="float64")[0]
print(f"||R||^2 = {normR2:.6f} (tau = 1 is valid since L = 1)")


def objective(x, yobs, Rk, eps):
    X = x.reshape(n, n)
    return 0.5 * np.linalg.norm(Rk @ x - yobs) ** 2 + eps * np.linalg.svd(X, compute_uv=False).sum()


def solve(yobs, idx_obs, eps, niter=300, track=False):
    Rk = Restriction(n * n, idx_obs, dtype="float64")
    proxf = L2(Op=Rk, b=yobs)  # grad = Rk^H (Rk x - y); no inner solve is needed
    proxg = Nuclear((n, n))
    hist = []
    cb = (lambda x: hist.append(objective(x, yobs, Rk, eps))) if track else None
    x = ProximalGradient(proxf, proxg, np.zeros(n * n), epsg=eps, tau=1.0,
                         acceleration="fista", niter=niter, callback=cb)
    return x, hist


# Truth-free choice of eps: hold out 20% of the observed entries and pick eps
# with the lowest validation error. The scale grid is set by the zero-filled data.
Y0 = np.zeros((n, n))
Y0.ravel()[idx] = y
scale = np.linalg.svd(Y0, compute_uv=False)[0]
eps_grid = np.logspace(-5, 0, 11) * scale

perm = np.random.default_rng(1).permutation(idx.size)
n_val = idx.size // 5
val, fit = perm[:n_val], perm[n_val:]
best_eps, best_err = None, np.inf
for eps in eps_grid:
    x, _ = solve(y[fit], idx[fit], eps)
    err = np.sqrt(np.mean((x[idx[val]] - y[val]) ** 2))
    print(f"eps={eps:9.3f}  validation RMSE={err:.4f}")
    if err < best_err:
        best_eps, best_err = eps, err
print(f"chosen eps = {best_eps:.3f} (validation RMSE {best_err:.4f})")

# Final solve on all observed entries, with the cost curve for the convergence check.
xinv, hist = solve(y, idx, best_eps, niter=500, track=True)
print(f"objective: start {hist[0]:.4f}, end {hist[-1]:.4f}, "
      f"last-50-iter relative change {abs(hist[-1] - hist[-50]) / abs(hist[-1]):.2e}")
n_up = int(np.sum(np.diff(hist) > 1e-8 * np.abs(hist[:-1])))
print(f"objective increases (FISTA is non-monotone): {n_up} of {len(hist) - 1} iterations")

Xinv = xinv.reshape(n, n)

# Baseline for context: fill missing entries with the observed mean.
Xbase = np.full((n, n), y.mean())
Xbase.ravel()[idx] = y
print(f"baseline (observed-mean fill) SNR_dB={metrics.snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xbase.ravel()):.2f}")
print(f"rank of Xinv (singular values > 1% of max): {np.sum(np.linalg.svd(Xinv, compute_uv=False) > 0.01 * np.linalg.norm(Xinv, 2))}")
print(f"SNR_DB={metrics.snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xinv.ravel())}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder, ran it, and it finishes with `SNR_DB=33.82959702053452`. That is the SNR of the full reconstructed 60 × 60 matrix against `Xtrue`.

**What I chose**

The underlying matrix is rank 3, so I treated this as low-rank matrix completion in two stages:

1. **Nuclear-norm completion with PyLops/PyProximal.** The data term is `pylops.Restriction` (masking to the observed entries) wrapped in `pyproximal.L2`. The prior is `pyproximal.proximal.Nuclear`, which soft-thresholds singular values. I solved it with FISTA via `pyproximal.optimization.primal.ProximalGradient`. This stage alone reaches about 20.7 dB. The leading singular values are recovered, but the nuclear norm shrinks them and leaves a tail of spurious small singular values.
2. **Rank-k refit by alternating least squares.** This step is plain numpy, not PyLops/PyProximal, because it needs no proximal operator. It refits the factors on the observed entries only, starting from the stage-1 solution. This removes the shrinkage and the spurious tail.

Both hyperparameters are chosen on a held-out 20% of the observed entries, never on `Xtrue`:
- the nuclear weight `lam` (chosen 0.01), and
- the rank `k` (chosen 3; the holdout RMSE is 0.066 at k=3 and rises to 0.11 at k=4 and 0.45 at k=6).

The final refit uses all observed entries.

**How well it worked**

- Final SNR: 33.8 dB. The recovered singular values are 80.4, 54.1, 42.8, against true values of 80.2, 54.1, 42.8.
- The holdout RMSE of 0.066 is close to the 0.05 noise level in the data.
- This is one seed. I did not test other seeds or other noise levels, so the 33.8 dB figure is for this synthetic setup only.

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr
from pyproximal import L2
from pyproximal.proximal import Nuclear
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ProximalGradient

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
n = 60
Xtrue = rng.standard_normal((n, 3)) @ rng.standard_normal((3, n))
idx = np.sort(rng.choice(n * n, int(0.3 * n * n), replace=False))  # observed entries (flat indices)
y = Xtrue.ravel()[idx] + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(idx.size)
rows, cols = np.divmod(idx, n)

# Stage 1: nuclear-norm matrix completion with PyProximal.
#   min_X 0.5 * ||R vec(X) - y||^2 + lam * ||X||_*
# R is a PyLops Restriction (masking) operator selecting the observed entries.
# Solved with accelerated proximal gradient (FISTA); the prox is the singular
# value soft-thresholding from pyproximal.proximal.Nuclear.


def nuclear_complete(idx_fit, y_fit, lam, niter=500):
    R = pylops.Restriction(n * n, idx_fit, dtype="float64")
    proxf = L2(Op=R, b=y_fit, sigma=1.0)  # Lipschitz constant of grad is ||R||^2 = 1
    proxg = Nuclear(dim=(n, n), sigma=lam)
    x0 = np.zeros(n * n)
    xinv = ProximalGradient(
        proxf, proxg, x0, tau=1.0, niter=niter, acceleration="fista", show=False
    )
    return xinv.reshape(n, n)


# Stage 2: debias at a fixed rank k by alternating least squares on the observed
# entries. The nuclear norm shrinks the leading singular values, and the rank
# of the stage-1 solution is inflated by spurious small singular values; a rank-k
# refit removes both effects. Plain numpy, initialized from the stage-1 solution.


def als_complete(rows_fit, cols_fit, y_fit, k, X_init, iters=50, reg=1e-6):
    U_s, s, Vh = np.linalg.svd(X_init, full_matrices=False)
    U = U_s[:, :k] * np.sqrt(s[:k])
    V = Vh[:k].T * np.sqrt(s[:k])
    for _ in range(iters):
        for i in range(n):
            m = rows_fit == i
            if m.any():
                A = V[cols_fit[m]]
                U[i] = np.linalg.solve(A.T @ A + reg * np.eye(k), A.T @ y_fit[m])
        for j in range(n):
            m = cols_fit == j
            if m.any():
                A = U[rows_fit[m]]
                V[j] = np.linalg.solve(A.T @ A + reg * np.eye(k), A.T @ y_fit[m])
    return U @ V.T


# Model selection uses only the observed entries: a held-out 20% split of them.
perm = rng.permutation(idx.size)
n_val = idx.size // 5
val, train = perm[:n_val], perm[n_val:]

lams = np.logspace(-3, 1, 9)
val_err = []
for lam in lams:
    Xh = nuclear_complete(idx[train], y[train], lam)
    val_err.append(np.sqrt(np.mean((Xh.ravel()[idx[val]] - y[val]) ** 2)))
    print(f"lam={lam:.4g}  holdout RMSE={val_err[-1]:.4f}")
lam_best = lams[int(np.argmin(val_err))]
print(f"chosen lam={lam_best:.4g}")

X_nuc_train = nuclear_complete(idx[train], y[train], lam_best, niter=1000)
ranks = range(1, 7)
rank_err = []
for k in ranks:
    Xk = als_complete(rows[train], cols[train], y[train], k, X_nuc_train)
    rank_err.append(np.sqrt(np.mean((Xk.ravel()[idx[val]] - y[val]) ** 2)))
    print(f"rank={k}  holdout RMSE={rank_err[-1]:.4f}")
k_best = list(ranks)[int(np.argmin(rank_err))]
print(f"chosen rank={k_best}")

# Refit on all observed entries with the chosen lam and rank.
X_nuc = nuclear_complete(idx, y, lam_best, niter=1000)
Xinv = als_complete(rows, cols, y, k_best, X_nuc, iters=100)
print(f"Xinv singular values (top 5): {np.round(np.linalg.svd(Xinv, compute_uv=False)[:5], 2)}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xinv.ravel())}")

I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder, ran it, and it finishes with `SNR_DB=33.82959702053452`. That is the SNR of the full reconstructed 60 × 60 matrix against `Xtrue`.

**What I chose**

The underlying matrix is rank 3, so I treated this as low-rank matrix completion in two stages:

1. **Nuclear-norm completion with PyLops/PyProximal.** The data term is `pylops.Restriction` (masking to the observed entries) wrapped in `pyproximal.L2`. The prior is `pyproximal.proximal.Nuclear`, which soft-thresholds singular values. I solved it with FISTA via `pyproximal.optimization.primal.ProximalGradient`. This stage alone reaches about 20.7 dB. The leading singular values are recovered, but the nuclear norm shrinks them and leaves a tail of spurious small singular values.
2. **Rank-k refit by alternating least squares.** This step is plain numpy, not PyLops/PyProximal, because it needs no proximal operator. It refits the factors on the observed entries only, starting from the stage-1 solution. This removes the shrinkage and the spurious tail.

Both hyperparameters are chosen on a held-out 20% of the observed entries, never on `Xtrue`:
- the nuclear weight `lam` (chosen 0.01), and
- the rank `k` (chosen 3; the holdout RMSE is 0.066 at k=3 and rises to 0.11 at k=4 and 0.45 at k=6).

The final refit uses all observed entries.

**How well it worked**

- Final SNR: 33.8 dB. The recovered singular values are 80.4, 54.1, 42.8, against true values of 80.2, 54.1, 42.8.
- The holdout RMSE of 0.066 is close to the 0.05 noise level in the data.
- This is one seed. I did not test other seeds or other noise levels, so the 33.8 dB figure is for this synthetic setup only.

from pylops.utils.metrics import snr
from pyproximal import L2

# value soft-thresholding from pyproximal.proximal.Nuclear.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The last line is `SNR_DB=26.50931511278805`, which is 26.5 dB on the full 60 × 60 reconstruction.

**What the script does**

1. **Convex nuclear-norm completion (PyLops + PyProximal):** `pylops.Restriction` picks out the observed entries, `pyproximal.L2` is the data-fit term, `pyproximal.Nuclear` is the low-rank penalty, and `pyproximal.optimization.primal.ProximalGradient` solves it with FISTA.
2. **Debiasing refit (NumPy):** the nuclear penalty shrinks the large singular values, so I keep the top-r column subspace of that solution and refit the coefficients by least squares on the observed entries.
3. **Hyperparameter choice without the true matrix:** `lam` and the rank `r` are chosen by validation MSE on a random 10% of the observed entries, then the model is refit on all observed entries.

The script selects `lam=0.1` and `rank=3`, which matches the true rank of the synthetic matrix. The selected `lam` sits in the interior of the widened grid (0.001 to 1), so it isn't pinned at an edge.

**How well it worked**

- A plain nuclear-norm solve gave only about 20 dB. The penalty shrinks the top singular values (for example 80 → 79 and 54 → 49), and it leaves a spurious small singular value where the truth is zero.
- The debiasing refit raised this to 26.5 dB.
- For reference, a rank-3 alternating least-squares fit on the observed entries reaches about 34 dB. That is not a PyLops/PyProximal method, and I didn't put it in the script. It suggests the convex relaxation's subspace estimate is the remaining limit.
- The validation MSE is about 0.030, well above the noise variance of 0.0025. That means held-out entries are still predicted with noticeable error, so the 26.5 dB figure is a reasonable result but not the noise-limited ceiling.

If you want closer to the ~34 dB reference, the next step would be to replace the nuclear-norm subspace estimate with a rank-constrained alternating solver, which is more code and not a PyProximal operator. Running the script takes about 8 seconds.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
import pyproximal
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
n = 60
Xtrue = rng.standard_normal((n, 3)) @ rng.standard_normal((3, n))
idx = np.sort(rng.choice(n * n, int(0.3 * n * n), replace=False))  # observed entries (flat indices)
y = Xtrue.ravel()[idx] + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(idx.size)


def nuclear_complete(iava, yobs, lam, niter=3000):
    """Convex nuclear-norm completion: min_X 0.5*||R(X)-y||^2 + lam*||X||_*, solved with FISTA."""
    R = pylops.Restriction(n * n, iava, dtype="float64")  # picks observed entries of vec(X)
    proxf = pyproximal.L2(Op=R, b=yobs, sigma=1.0)
    proxg = pyproximal.Nuclear((n, n), sigma=1.0)
    x = pyproximal.optimization.primal.ProximalGradient(
        proxf, proxg, np.zeros(n * n), epsg=lam, tau=1.0, niter=niter, acceleration="fista"
    )
    return x.reshape(n, n)


def debias(Xnuc, iava, yobs, r):
    """Keep the top-r column subspace of the nuclear solution, then refit the coefficients
    by least squares on the observed entries to remove the shrinkage bias of the penalty."""
    M = np.full((n, n), np.nan)
    M.ravel()[iava] = yobs
    mask = ~np.isnan(M)
    U = np.linalg.svd(Xnuc, full_matrices=False)[0][:, :r]
    A = np.zeros((r, n))
    for j in range(n):
        rows = np.where(mask[:, j])[0]
        A[:, j] = np.linalg.lstsq(U[rows], M[rows, j], rcond=None)[0]
    return U @ A


# Select lam and rank on a validation split of the observed entries (true matrix not used)
perm = rng.permutation(idx.size)
nval = idx.size // 10
val_pos, fit_pos = perm[:nval], perm[nval:]
iava_fit, y_fit = idx[fit_pos], y[fit_pos]
iava_val, y_val = idx[val_pos], y[val_pos]

best = (np.inf, None, None)
for lam in np.logspace(-3, 0, 7):
    Xnuc = nuclear_complete(iava_fit, y_fit, lam)
    for r in range(2, 7):
        Xr = debias(Xnuc, iava_fit, y_fit, r)
        err = np.mean((Xr.ravel()[iava_val] - y_val) ** 2)
        if err < best[0]:
            best = (err, lam, r)
best_err, best_lam, best_r = best

# Refit on all observed entries with the selected lam and rank
Xnuc = nuclear_complete(idx, y, best_lam)
Xinv = debias(Xnuc, idx, y, best_r)

print(f"selected lam={best_lam:.4g}, rank={best_r}, validation MSE={best_err:.4g}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xinv.ravel())}")

        err = np.mean((Xr.ravel()[iava_val] - y_val) ** 2)
        if err < best[0]:

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder, ran it, and it prints `SNR_DB=22.606350189101203` as its last line. That's 22.6 dB on the full 60 × 60 matrix, against a noise level of 0.05 on the observed entries.

**What I chose**

Nuclear-norm matrix completion, since the underlying table is rank 3:

- **Solver:** PyProximal `ProximalGradient` with FISTA acceleration, 3000 iterations.
- **Data term:** `0.5·||R·vec(X) − y||²`, where `R` is a PyLops `Restriction` to the observed entries.
- **Regularizer:** `λ·||X||_*`, via PyProximal's `Nuclear` prox.
- **Choosing λ:** I held out 20% of the observed entries and picked λ by validation RMSE, so the reconstruction never looks at `Xtrue`. The grid ran from 1e-4 to 0.3, and λ = 1e-3 won.
- **Debiasing:** the nuclear-norm solution shrinks the singular values. I kept the leading singular subspace, with rank chosen at the largest singular-value gap (rank 3), and refit the 3×3 core by least squares on the observed entries only.

**How it worked**

| Step | SNR (dB) |
|---|---|
| Nuclear norm only (λ = 1e-3) | 20.96 |
| After rank-3 debiasing (final) | 22.61 |

The relative error is 0.074. The SNR metric comes from `pylops.utils.metrics.snr`, and the script is deterministic (seed 0).

**Caveats**

- The gain from debiasing is modest, about 1.6 dB. I included it because the rank was chosen from observed data alone.
- The result is limited by how the rank-3 prior is imposed. A fit that knows the true rank could probably do better; I didn't test that.
- The λ choice barely matters: validation RMSE stays near 0.37–0.38 across most of the grid.
- Validation RMSE (about 0.37) is well above the 0.05 noise, so the completion error, not measurement noise, dominates.

- **Data term:** `0.5·||R·vec(X) − y||²`, where `R` is a PyLops `Restriction` to the observed entries.
- **Regularizer:** `λ·||X||_*`, via PyProximal's `Nuclear` prox.
- **Choosing λ:** I held out 20% of the observed entries and picked λ by validation RMSE, so the reconstruction never looks at `Xtrue`. The grid ran from 1e-4 to 0.3, and λ = 1e-3 won.
- **Debiasing:** the nuclear-norm solution shrinks the singular values. I kept the leading singular subspace, with rank chosen at the largest singular-value gap (rank 3), and refit the 3×3 core by least squares on the observed entries only.

**How it worked**

| Step | SNR (dB) |
|---|---|
| Nuclear norm only (λ = 1e-3) | 20.96 |
| After rank-3 debiasing (final) | 22.61 |

The relative error is 0.074. The SNR metric comes from `pylops.utils.metrics.snr`, and the script is deterministic (seed 0).

**Caveats**

- The gain from debiasing is modest, about 1.6 dB. I included it because the rank was chosen from observed data alone.
- The result is limited by how the rank-3 prior is imposed. A fit that knows the true rank could probably do better; I didn't test that.
- The λ choice barely matters: validation RMSE stays near 0.37–0.38 across most of the grid.
- Validation RMSE (about 0.37) is well above the 0.05 noise, so the completion error, not measurement noise, dominates.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.125

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr
from pyproximal import L2
from pyproximal.proximal import Nuclear
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ProximalGradient

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
n = 60
Xtrue = rng.standard_normal((n, 3)) @ rng.standard_normal((3, n))
idx = np.sort(rng.choice(n * n, int(0.3 * n * n), replace=False))  # observed entries (flat indices)
y = Xtrue.ravel()[idx] + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(idx.size)


def complete(iava, yobs, lam, niter=3000):
    # min_X 0.5 * ||R vec(X) - yobs||^2 + lam * ||X||_*  (low-rank matrix completion)
    Rop = pylops.Restriction(n * n, iava, dtype="float64")
    proxf = L2(Op=Rop, b=yobs, sigma=1.0)
    proxg = Nuclear(dim=(n, n), sigma=1.0)
    x0 = np.zeros(n * n)
    xinv = ProximalGradient(proxf, proxg, x0, epsg=lam, tau=1.0,
                            niter=niter, acceleration="fista")
    return xinv.reshape(n, n)


# Choose lam on a held-out 20% of the observed entries (no access to Xtrue)
perm = rng.permutation(idx.size)
nval = idx.size // 5
val, tr = perm[:nval], perm[nval:]
best_lam, best_err = None, np.inf
for lam in [1e-4, 1e-3, 3e-3, 1e-2, 3e-2, 1e-1, 3e-1]:
    Xtr = complete(idx[tr], y[tr], lam)
    err = np.sqrt(np.mean((Xtr.ravel()[idx[val]] - y[val]) ** 2))
    print(f"lam={lam:<5} val_rmse={err:.4f}")
    if err < best_err:
        best_lam, best_err = lam, err
print(f"selected lam={best_lam}")

# Refit on all observed entries with the selected lam
Xnuc = complete(idx, y, best_lam)
print(f"nuclear_only_snr_db={snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xnuc.ravel()):.3f}")


def debias(X, y, iobs, nmax=10):
    # Nuclear-norm shrinkage biases the singular values. Keep the leading subspace,
    # choosing the rank at the largest singular-value gap, then least-squares refit
    # the core matrix M in X = U M V^T on the observed entries only.
    U, s, Vt = np.linalg.svd(X)
    k = int(np.argmax(s[:nmax - 1] / s[1:nmax])) + 1
    U, V = U[:, :k], Vt[:k].T
    rows, cols = iobs // n, iobs % n
    A = np.einsum("ni,nj->nij", U[rows], V[cols]).reshape(iobs.size, k * k)
    M = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)[0].reshape(k, k)
    return U @ M @ V.T, k


Xinv, rank = debias(Xnuc, y, idx)
print(f"debiased_rank={rank}")
print(f"relative_error={np.linalg.norm(Xinv - Xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(Xtrue):.4f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(Xtrue.ravel(), Xinv.ravel())}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder, ran it, and it recovers the image at **SNR = 17.60 dB**, against 16.44 dB for a plain least-squares baseline. The last line is `SNR_DB=17.602337381736234`.

**Formulation.** `min_x ½‖Op x − y‖² + g(x)`, where `g` is implicit: its proximal step is the 3×3 median filter.

**Solver.** `pyproximal.optimization.pnp.PlugAndPlay` with `solver=ProximalGradient`. The data term is `pyproximal.L2(Op=Op, b=y)`, which has a gradient. The denoiser runs at every iteration, not as a post-processing step. I chose this over ADMM because the data term is smooth and cheap to differentiate, so no inner solve is needed.

**Step size.** `L = ‖Op‖²` estimated by power iteration (100 iterations), inflated by 5%. That gives `L ≈ 1.039` and `tau = 1/L ≈ 0.962`. The 5% inflation is a safety margin I added on top of the bound; it is not derived from the median filter, which is not a true proximal operator.

**Free parameters and how they were chosen, without the true image:**
- **Stopping iteration:** the discrepancy principle. I stop at the first iterate whose residual `‖Op x − y‖` drops to `σ√N = 0.64`, with `σ = 0.01` as the nominal noise level. This stopped at iteration 46.
- **`tau`:** fixed at `1/L` for step-size stability, not tuned. The median filter has no strength parameter, so `tau` is the only knob the solver sees, and the denoiser ignores it.
- **Median size:** fixed at 3, as you specified.

**Convergence evidence.** The callback records each iterate:
- Data cost went from 4.26 at the start to 0.205 at the stopping iteration (0.183 at iteration 200).
- Relative change at iteration 200 is 2.8e-4, so the iteration settles to a fixed point.
- The residual drops below the noise level at iteration 46 and stays below it afterwards (0.605 at iteration 200), so the discrepancy rule is what determines the reported image.

**Baseline.** CGLS with the same discrepancy stopping rule gives 16.44 dB. The gain from the prior is about 1.2 dB, which is modest for a blocky image.

**Caveats:**
- PnP has no explicit objective, so the "cost" printed is only the data term. There is no objective whose decrease proves the method converged.
- The discrepancy rule needs the noise level. Here `σ = 0.01` is the value used to generate the data; on real data you would need to estimate it.
- I did not tune the median filter's effect beyond the stopping rule, and I did not check sensitivity to `tau` or the step-size inflation.

I did not commit anything; the only file created is `/private/tmp/e-aKfYVw/home/cwd/invert.py`.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
import pyproximal
from pylops.optimization.basic import cgls
from pylops.optimization.eigs import power_iteration
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr
from pyproximal.optimization.pnp import PlugAndPlay
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ProximalGradient
from scipy.ndimage import median_filter

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))
xtrue[8:56, 8:56] = 0.3
xtrue[16:30, 14:40] = 1.0
xtrue[36:50, 20:32] = 0.7
xtrue[34:52, 40:50] = 0.5
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:ny, :nx]
xtrue[(yy - 24) ** 2 + (xx - 46) ** 2 < 36] = 0.8

t = np.arange(-10, 11)
g = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 1.5) ** 2)
h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma = 0.01  # nominal noise std (known from acquisition, not from xtrue)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + sigma * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)
N = ny * nx

# Objective (implicit prior):
#   min_x  0.5 * ||Op x - y||^2 + g(x),   g implicit: prox_{tau g} = 3x3 median filter
# Solver: ProximalGradient (f smooth, gradient step on the data term, then
# denoiser step), wrapped by PlugAndPlay.

# Step size: tau <= 1/L with L = ||Op||^2 (estimated, inflated by 5 %)
L = 1.05 * power_iteration(Op.H @ Op, niter=100)[0]
tau = 1.0 / L


def denoise(x, tau):
    # 3x3 median filter; the tau argument is unused (the median has no strength knob)
    return median_filter(x, size=3, mode="reflect")


# Truth-free stopping rule (discrepancy principle): stop at the first iterate
# whose data residual ||Op x - y|| is at the noise level sigma * sqrt(N)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(N)

iterates = []


def store(x):
    iterates.append(x.copy())


proxf = pyproximal.L2(Op=Op, b=y)
xpnp_last = PlugAndPlay(
    proxf,
    denoise,
    dims=(ny, nx),
    x0=np.zeros(N),
    solver=ProximalGradient,
    tau=tau,
    epsg=1.0,
    niter=200,
    callback=store,
)

res_pnp = np.array([np.linalg.norm(Op @ it - y.ravel()) for it in iterates])
ok = np.where(res_pnp <= target)[0]
k_pnp = ok[0] if len(ok) else len(iterates) - 1
xinv = iterates[k_pnp].reshape(ny, nx)

data_cost = lambda x: 0.5 * np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y.ravel()) ** 2
rel_change = np.linalg.norm(iterates[-1] - iterates[-2]) / np.linalg.norm(iterates[-2])

print(f"L = ||Op||^2 (inflated) = {L:.6f}, tau = 1/L = {tau:.6f}")
print(f"target residual (discrepancy) = {target:.4f}")
print(f"PnP: iterations run = {len(iterates)}, stopped at iter {k_pnp + 1}")
print(f"PnP: data cost start = {data_cost(iterates[0]):.4f}, "
      f"at stop = {data_cost(xinv.ravel()):.4f}, end = {data_cost(iterates[-1]):.4f}")
print(f"PnP: relative change at last iter = {rel_change:.3e}")
print(f"PnP: residual at stop = {res_pnp[k_pnp]:.4f}, last = {res_pnp[-1]:.4f}")

# Baseline: unregularised least squares (CGLS), same discrepancy stopping rule
xls = None
for k in range(1, 201):
    xk = cgls(Op, y.ravel(), niter=k)[0].reshape(ny, nx)
    if np.linalg.norm(Op @ xk.ravel() - y.ravel()) <= target:
        xls = xk
        break
if xls is None:
    xls = xk
print(f"Baseline CGLS (discrepancy-stopped): SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xls):.4f}")

print(f"PnP median-filter prior: SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={float(snr(xtrue, xinv))}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder, ran it, and it recovers the image at **SNR = 17.60 dB**, against 16.44 dB for a plain least-squares baseline. The last line is `SNR_DB=17.602337381736234`.

**Formulation.** `min_x ½‖Op x − y‖² + g(x)`, where `g` is implicit: its proximal step is the 3×3 median filter.

**Solver.** `pyproximal.optimization.pnp.PlugAndPlay` with `solver=ProximalGradient`. The data term is `pyproximal.L2(Op=Op, b=y)`, which has a gradient. The denoiser runs at every iteration, not as a post-processing step. I chose this over ADMM because the data term is smooth and cheap to differentiate, so no inner solve is needed.

**Step size.** `L = ‖Op‖²` estimated by power iteration (100 iterations), inflated by 5%. That gives `L ≈ 1.039` and `tau = 1/L ≈ 0.962`. The 5% inflation is a safety margin I added on top of the bound; it is not derived from the median filter, which is not a true proximal operator.

**Free parameters and how they were chosen, without the true image:**
- **Stopping iteration:** the discrepancy principle. I stop at the first iterate whose residual `‖Op x − y‖` drops to `σ√N = 0.64`, with `σ = 0.01` as the nominal noise level. This stopped at iteration 46.
- **`tau`:** fixed at `1/L` for step-size stability, not tuned. The median filter has no strength parameter, so `tau` is the only knob the solver sees, and the denoiser ignores it.
- **Median size:** fixed at 3, as you specified.

**Convergence evidence.** The callback records each iterate:
- Data cost went from 4.26 at the start to 0.205 at the stopping iteration (0.183 at iteration 200).
- Relative change at iteration 200 is 2.8e-4, so the iteration settles to a fixed point.
- The residual drops below the noise level at iteration 46 and stays below it afterwards (0.605 at iteration 200), so the discrepancy rule is what determines the reported image.

**Baseline.** CGLS with the same discrepancy stopping rule gives 16.44 dB. The gain from the prior is about 1.2 dB, which is modest for a blocky image.

**Caveats:**
- PnP has no explicit objective, so the "cost" printed is only the data term. There is no objective whose decrease proves the method converged.
- The discrepancy rule needs the noise level. Here `σ = 0.01` is the value used to generate the data; on real data you would need to estimate it.
- I did not tune the median filter's effect beyond the stopping rule, and I did not check sensitivity to `tau` or the step-size inflation.

I did not commit anything; the only file created is `/private/tmp/e-aKfYVw/home/cwd/invert.py`.

import numpy as np
import pylops
import pyproximal
from pylops.optimization.basic import cgls
from pylops.optimization.eigs import power_iteration
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr
from pyproximal.optimization.pnp import PlugAndPlay
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ProximalGradient
from scipy.ndimage import median_filter

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))
xtrue[8:56, 8:56] = 0.3
xtrue[16:30, 14:40] = 1.0
xtrue[36:50, 20:32] = 0.7
xtrue[34:52, 40:50] = 0.5
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:ny, :nx]
xtrue[(yy - 24) ** 2 + (xx - 46) ** 2 < 36] = 0.8

t = np.arange(-10, 11)
g = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 1.5) ** 2)
h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma = 0.01  # nominal noise std (known from acquisition, not from xtrue)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + sigma * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)
N = ny * nx

# Objective (implicit prior):
#   min_x  0.5 * ||Op x - y||^2 + g(x),   g implicit: prox_{tau g} = 3x3 median filter
# Solver: ProximalGradient (f smooth, gradient step on the data term, then
# denoiser step), wrapped by PlugAndPlay.

# Step size: tau <= 1/L with L = ||Op||^2 (estimated, inflated by 5 %)
L = 1.05 * power_iteration(Op.H @ Op, niter=100)[0]
tau = 1.0 / L


def denoise(x, tau):
    # 3x3 median filter; the tau argument is unused (the median has no strength knob)
    return median_filter(x, size=3, mode="reflect")


# Truth-free stopping rule (discrepancy principle): stop at the first iterate
# whose data residual ||Op x - y|| is at the noise level sigma * sqrt(N)
target = sigma * np.sqrt(N)

iterates = []


def store(x):
    iterates.append(x.copy())


proxf = pyproximal.L2(Op=Op, b=y)
xpnp_last = PlugAndPlay(
    proxf,
    denoise,
    dims=(ny, nx),
    x0=np.zeros(N),
    solver=ProximalGradient,
    tau=tau,
    epsg=1.0,
    niter=200,
    callback=store,
)

res_pnp = np.array([np.linalg.norm(Op @ it - y.ravel()) for it in iterates])
ok = np.where(res_pnp <= target)[0]
k_pnp = ok[0] if len(ok) else len(iterates) - 1
xinv = iterates[k_pnp].reshape(ny, nx)

data_cost = lambda x: 0.5 * np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y.ravel()) ** 2
rel_change = np.linalg.norm(iterates[-1] - iterates[-2]) / np.linalg.norm(iterates[-2])

print(f"L = ||Op||^2 (inflated) = {L:.6f}, tau = 1/L = {tau:.6f}")
print(f"target residual (discrepancy) = {target:.4f}")
print(f"PnP: iterations run = {len(iterates)}, stopped at iter {k_pnp + 1}")
print(f"PnP: data cost start = {data_cost(iterates[0]):.4f}, "
      f"at stop = {data_cost(xinv.ravel()):.4f}, end = {data_cost(iterates[-1]):.4f}")
print(f"PnP: relative change at last iter = {rel_change:.3e}")
print(f"PnP: residual at stop = {res_pnp[k_pnp]:.4f}, last = {res_pnp[-1]:.4f}")

# Baseline: unregularised least squares (CGLS), same discrepancy stopping rule
xls = None
for k in range(1, 201):
    xk = cgls(Op, y.ravel(), niter=k)[0].reshape(ny, nx)
    if np.linalg.norm(Op @ xk.ravel() - y.ravel()) <= target:
        xls = xk
        break
if xls is None:
    xls = xk
print(f"Baseline CGLS (discrepancy-stopped): SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xls):.4f}")

print(f"PnP median-filter prior: SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")
print(f"SNR_DB={float(snr(xtrue, xinv))}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the working folder, ran it, and the last line is `SNR_DB=17.92521367619296`.

**What I chose**

- **Objective as written:** `min_x ½‖Op x − y‖² + R(x)`, where `R` is implicit. Its proximal step is replaced by a 3×3 median filter, which is plug-and-play.
- **Solver:** `pyproximal.optimization.pnp.PlugAndPlay` with ADMM. The data term is `pyproximal.L2(Op=Op, b=y, niter=20, warm=True)`, and the z-update is `median_filter(x, size=3)`.
- **Penalty `tau`:** chosen by the discrepancy principle. I picked the value where the data residual RMS matches the noise level of 0.01 from your setup. I treated that noise level as a known input and did not use `xtrue` for tuning.
- **Step sizes:** ADMM has no operator-norm step condition, so only `tau > 0` matters here. The median filter has no strength parameter, so `tau` is the only tuned quantity.
- **Iterations:** 150, fixed.

**How it worked**

- The chosen `tau` is 0.316. The data residual RMS is 0.0101 against a target of 0.01.
- Convergence: the relative change of `z` falls from 4.3e-1 on the first iteration to 2.8e-4 on the last.
- Baseline: a Tikhonov-damped least-squares solve with the damping chosen by the same discrepancy rule gives 16.78 dB. The PnP result is about 1.1 dB better.
- The tau scan also printed SNR as a diagnostic only. It peaks at about 20.7 dB near `tau ≈ 3`, so the truth-free choice leaves about 2.8 dB on the table. I did not use that value, because choosing it would require the true image.

**Caveats**

- A median filter is not a proximal operator, so PnP-ADMM has no convergence guarantee here. The decreasing change in `z` is an empirical check only.
- The discrepancy rule depends on knowing the noise level. On real data you would need the instrument noise estimate.

**Fixes during the run:** the first version had the wrong `lsqr` import path for PyLops 2.8, and the baseline returned a 2-D array that needed flattening. Both are fixed in the file.

"""Plug-and-play deblurring of a blocky image with a 3x3 median filter prior.

Problem (as written):
    min_x  1/2 ||Op x - y||^2 + R(x)
where R is implicit: its proximal step is replaced by a 3x3 median filter
(plug-and-play). Solved with ADMM splitting x = z:
    x-update: prox of 1/2 ||Op x - y||^2 with penalty tau   (inner CG via pyproximal.L2)
    z-update: median3x3(x + u)                              (denoiser, tau unused)
    u-update: u + x - z
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
import pyproximal
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pyproximal.optimization.pnp import PlugAndPlay
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ADMM
from scipy.ndimage import median_filter

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))
xtrue[8:56, 8:56] = 0.3
xtrue[16:30, 14:40] = 1.0
xtrue[36:50, 20:32] = 0.7
xtrue[34:52, 40:50] = 0.5
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:ny, :nx]
xtrue[(yy - 24) ** 2 + (xx - 46) ** 2 < 36] = 0.8

t = np.arange(-10, 11)
g = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 1.5) ** 2)
h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)

# Noise standard deviation: an input of the problem (instrument level), not
# derived from xtrue. Used only by the discrepancy principle below.
sigma_noise = 0.01
dims = (ny, nx)
x0 = np.zeros(ny * nx)


def rms(v):
    return np.sqrt(np.mean(v**2))


def denoiser(x, tau):
    # 3x3 median filter: it has no noise-level parameter, so tau is not used
    return median_filter(x, size=3)


def run_pnp(tau, niter, callback=None):
    # fresh data-term object per run: L2 with warm=True keeps state between calls
    proxf = pyproximal.L2(Op=Op, b=y, niter=20, warm=True)
    return PlugAndPlay(
        proxf,
        denoiser,
        dims,
        x0,
        solver=ADMM,
        tau=tau,
        niter=niter,
        callback=callback,
        callbackz=True,
    )


NITER = 150

# 1) choose the ADMM penalty tau with the discrepancy principle:
#    pick tau so that the data residual RMS matches the noise level.
taus = np.logspace(-2, 1, 7)
print("tau scan (discrepancy principle; SNR column is diagnostic only)")
resids = []
for tau in taus:
    _, z_s = run_pnp(tau, NITER)
    r = rms(Op @ z_s - y)
    resids.append(r)
    print(
        f"  tau={tau:8.4f}  rms(Op z - y)={r:.4f}  "
        f"snr_diag={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, z_s.reshape(ny, nx)):.2f}"
    )
resids = np.array(resids)
idx = np.argmin(np.abs(np.log(resids) - np.log(sigma_noise)))
tau_best = taus[idx]
print(f"chosen tau={tau_best:.4f} (rms residual {resids[idx]:.4f}, target {sigma_noise})")

# 2) final run with convergence history
hist_dz = []
z_prev = [None]


def callback(x, z):
    if z_prev[0] is not None:
        hist_dz.append(np.linalg.norm(z - z_prev[0]) / np.linalg.norm(z))
    z_prev[0] = z.copy()


_, z_final = run_pnp(tau_best, NITER, callback=callback)
xinv = z_final.reshape(ny, nx)

print(
    f"ADMM iterations: {NITER}; relative change of z per iteration: "
    f"first={hist_dz[0]:.3e}, last={hist_dz[-1]:.3e}"
)
print(f"final data residual rms(Op xinv - y)={rms(Op @ z_final - y):.4f}")

# 3) baseline: Tikhonov-damped least squares, damp chosen by the same principle
best_d, best_err, best_x = None, np.inf, None
for d in np.logspace(-3, 1, 9):
    xd = lsqr(Op, y, damp=d, niter=300)[0].ravel()
    err = abs(np.log(rms(Op @ xd - y)) - np.log(sigma_noise))
    if err < best_err:
        best_d, best_err, best_x = d, err, xd
print(
    f"baseline Tikhonov damp={best_d:.4g}: "
    f"SNR={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, best_x.reshape(ny, nx)):.2f} dB"
)

snr_db = pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr_db}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. The last line is `SNR_DB=20.41092809573997`, against 13.93 dB for a 30-iteration least-squares baseline.

**Objective (as written):** `min_x ½‖Op x − y‖² + λ·g(x)`, where `g` is implicit: the ADMM z-update is replaced by a 3×3 median filter (plug-and-play prior).

**Solver:** `pyproximal.pnp.PlugAndPlay` with `ADMM`. The data term is `L2` with the blur inside, using pyproximal's inner solve (`niter=20`, `warm=True`). I did not use `ProximalGradient` because the median filter is applied to the z-variable, which is the standard PnP-ADMM structure. The median filter has no strength parameter, so `tau` (the ADMM penalty) is the only free parameter.

**Step sizes:** ADMM has no step-size bound on `tau`, so there is nothing to derive. Convergence of PnP with a median denoiser is not guaranteed by theory, so I checked it empirically instead (see below).

**Parameter choice (truth-free):** 20% of the observed samples are held out. For each `tau` on a log grid from 0.01 to 100, I fit on the remaining 80% and measured the held-out data MSE. The selected `tau = 10` has held-out MSE 1.19e-4, with 3.2 and 31.6 nearly tied and the grid extremes clearly worse. The true image is not used for the solve or the selection.

**Convergence evidence:**
- At 100 iterations, `‖x − z‖` drops from 0.42 to 0.0088, and the relative data residual is 2.3e-2.
- At 400 iterations, `‖x − z‖` is 0.022, so the residual is not monotone. The SNR is unchanged (20.42 dB), so the result is stable in practice, but I would not call the splitting converged in the strict sense.

**Issue found and worked around:** `pylops.Restriction(N, idx) @ Op` returns a `(64, 64)` shape from `.H @ b`, which breaks `L2`'s internal `OpTb`. I wrapped the operator in `pylops.FunctionOperator` to force flat vectors. This is a pylops/pyproximal interaction, not a change to the installed packages.

**Parameters left to tune:** the number of iterations (100 is the value I used), the held-out fraction and `tau` grid, and the inner `niter` of the L2 prox. The median filter size is fixed at 3×3 as you specified.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""Plug-and-play deblurring of a blocky image with a 3x3 median filter prior.

Problem (written as the PnP-ADMM target):
    min_x  1/2 ||Op x - y||^2  +  lam * g(x),
where g is implicit: the z-update of ADMM is replaced by a 3x3 median filter
(plug-and-play priors, Venkatakrishnan et al. 2013).

Solver: pyproximal.pnp.PlugAndPlay with ADMM; proxf is pyproximal.L2 with
the blur operator inside (inner solve, warm-started). The ADMM penalty tau is
the only free parameter; it is chosen on a held-out subset of y, without the
true image.
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
import pyproximal
from pyproximal.optimization.pnp import PlugAndPlay
from pyproximal.optimization.primal import ADMM
from scipy.ndimage import median_filter
from scipy.sparse.linalg import lsqr

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))
xtrue[8:56, 8:56] = 0.3
xtrue[16:30, 14:40] = 1.0
xtrue[36:50, 20:32] = 0.7
xtrue[34:52, 40:50] = 0.5
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:ny, :nx]
xtrue[(yy - 24) ** 2 + (xx - 46) ** 2 < 36] = 0.8

t = np.arange(-10, 11)
g = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 1.5) ** 2)
h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)
N = ny * nx

# Truth-free split: 20% of the data are held out for choosing tau
perm = np.random.default_rng(1).permutation(N)
iva = np.sort(perm[: N // 5])
itr = np.sort(perm[N // 5 :])


def denoiser(x, tau):
    """3x3 median filter applied to x reshaped to the image; tau is unused
    (the median filter has no strength parameter)."""
    return median_filter(x.reshape(ny, nx), size=3).ravel()


def pnp_admm(tau, iidx, niter):
    """PnP-ADMM fitting only the data at indices iidx."""
    R = pylops.Restriction(N, iidx, dtype="float64")
    RO = R @ Op
    # wrap with matvec/rmatvec: `R @ Op` returns (ny, nx) from `.H @ b`,
    # which breaks L2's internal OpTb (flat vectors are needed)
    Op_fit = pylops.FunctionOperator(
        RO.matvec, RO.rmatvec, len(iidx), N, dtype="float64"
    )
    proxf = pyproximal.L2(Op=Op_fit, b=y[iidx], niter=20, warm=True)
    hist = []

    def callback(x, z):
        # primal residual ||x - z|| per iteration (convergence of the splitting)
        hist.append(np.linalg.norm(x - z))

    x, z = PlugAndPlay(
        proxf,
        denoiser,
        dims=(ny, nx),
        x0=np.zeros(N),
        solver=ADMM,
        tau=tau,
        niter=niter,
        callback=callback,
        callbackz=True,
    )
    return x, z, np.array(hist)


# 1) choose tau on the held-out part of the data (no true image used)
taus = np.logspace(-2, 2, 9)
niter_sel = 60
print("tau selection (held-out data MSE, truth-free):")
best_tau, best_mse = None, np.inf
for tau in taus:
    x_sel, _, _ = pnp_admm(tau, itr, niter_sel)
    mse = np.mean((Op @ x_sel - y)[iva] ** 2)
    print(f"  tau={tau:8.3f}  held-out MSE={mse:.3e}")
    if mse < best_mse:
        best_tau, best_mse = tau, mse
print(f"selected tau={best_tau:.3f}")

# 2) final run with the selected tau on all data
niter = 100
xinv, zinv, hist = pnp_admm(best_tau, np.arange(N), niter)
print(f"iterations={niter}, ||x-z|| start={hist[0]:.3e}, end={hist[-1]:.3e}")
print(f"final data residual ||Op x - y||/||y|| = "
      f"{np.linalg.norm(Op @ xinv - y) / np.linalg.norm(y):.3e}")
xinv = xinv.reshape(ny, nx)

# baseline: plain least squares, early-stopped (no prior)
xls = lsqr(Op, y, iter_lim=30)[0].reshape(ny, nx)
print(f"SNR baseline (lsqr, 30 it) = {pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")

print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder, ran it, and it prints `SNR_DB=20.256423775850276`. The reconstruction is `xinv`, a 64×64 array.

**What the script does** (PnP-ADMM, plug-and-play ADMM):
- **Data term:** the x-step solves `(AᵀA + ρI) x = Aᵀy + ρ(z − u)` with pylops' conjugate gradient (`pylops.optimization.basic.cg`, 20 iterations per step, warm-started).
- **Prior:** the z-step replaces the prior's proximal step with a 3×3 median filter applied to `x + u` (`scipy.ndimage.median_filter`, `mode="reflect"`).
- **Dual update:** `u ← u + x − z`.
- **Settings:** ρ = 0.1, 50 outer iterations. The output is the final `z`, since that is the iterate that has been through the median prior.

**How well it worked:** on this synthetic image, the SNR is 20.3 dB. For reference, I ran a throwaway comparison (not saved in the folder):
- Blurred adjoint `Aᵀy`: 12.9 dB
- Tikhonov (plain CG-solved ridge) with the best of three λ values tried: 16.8 dB (λ = 0.01)

So the median-prior inversion is about 3.5 dB better than the best Tikhonov I tried. Caveat: I picked ρ = 0.1 and the iteration counts without tuning them, so the 20.3 dB is not an optimised number. The Tikhonov sweep did look at the ground truth to choose λ, which makes that baseline slightly favourable to Tikhonov, not to the PnP method. If you want a fairer comparison, sweep ρ and the iteration count on a separate noise realisation rather than the one you report.

import numpy as np
import pylops
from scipy.ndimage import median_filter
from pylops.optimization.basic import cg

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))
xtrue[8:56, 8:56] = 0.3
xtrue[16:30, 14:40] = 1.0
xtrue[36:50, 20:32] = 0.7
xtrue[34:52, 40:50] = 0.5
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:ny, :nx]
xtrue[(yy - 24) ** 2 + (xx - 46) ** 2 < 36] = 0.8

t = np.arange(-10, 11)
g = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 1.5) ** 2)
h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)

# Plug-and-Play ADMM: data term handled exactly through the operator,
# prior handled by the 3x3 median filter acting as the denoiser.
rho = 0.1
niter = 50
cg_iter = 20

def denoise(v):
    return median_filter(v.reshape(ny, nx), size=3, mode="reflect").ravel()

x = Op.H @ y
z = x.copy()
u = np.zeros_like(x)

# Normal operator (A^H A + rho I), used in the x-update
NormOp = Op.H * Op + rho * pylops.Identity(ny * nx)

for _ in range(niter):
    # x-update: argmin 0.5||Ax - y||^2 + rho/2 ||x - z + u||^2
    rhs = Op.H @ y + rho * (z - u)
    x = cg(NormOp, rhs, x0=x, niter=cg_iter, tol=1e-10, show=False)[0]
    # z-update: prior step, median filter applied to x + u
    z = denoise(x + u)
    # dual update
    u = u + x - z

# Final estimate: the denoised iterate, which satisfies the prior
xinv = z.reshape(ny, nx)

print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

NormOp = Op.H * Op + rho * pylops.Identity(ny * nx)

for _ in range(niter):

    rhs = Op.H @ y + rho * (z - u)
    x = cg(NormOp, rhs, x0=x, niter=cg_iter, tol=1e-10, show=False)[0]
    # z-update: prior step, median filter applied to x + u
    z = denoise(x + u)
    # dual update
    u = u + x - z

# Final estimate: the denoised iterate, which satisfies the prior
xinv = z.reshape(ny, nx)

print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

**What the script does**

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from scipy.ndimage import median_filter
from pylops.optimization.basic import cg

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))
xtrue[8:56, 8:56] = 0.3
xtrue[16:30, 14:40] = 1.0
xtrue[36:50, 20:32] = 0.7
xtrue[34:52, 40:50] = 0.5
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:ny, :nx]
xtrue[(yy - 24) ** 2 + (xx - 46) ** 2 < 36] = 0.8

t = np.arange(-10, 11)
g = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 1.5) ** 2)
h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)

# Plug-and-play ADMM: the median filter replaces the proximal operator of the prior
rho = 0.1      # ADMM penalty parameter
niter = 30     # PnP-ADMM outer iterations
ncg = 20       # CG iterations for each x-update

Iden = pylops.Identity(ny * nx)
normal = Op.H @ Op + rho * Iden   # x-update system: (A^H A + rho I) x = A^H y + rho (z - u)
aty = Op.H @ y

x = np.zeros(ny * nx)
z = np.zeros(ny * nx)
u = np.zeros(ny * nx)
for _ in range(niter):
    # x-update: data fit + quadratic coupling to the denoised variable
    x = cg(normal, aty + rho * (z - u), x0=x, niter=ncg)[0]
    # z-update: 3x3 median filter as the denoiser prior
    z = median_filter((x + u).reshape(ny, nx), size=3, mode="reflect").ravel()
    # dual update
    u = u + x - z

xinv = z.reshape(ny, nx)
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

import numpy as np
import pylops
from scipy.ndimage import median_filter
from pylops.optimization.basic import cg

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))
xtrue[8:56, 8:56] = 0.3
xtrue[16:30, 14:40] = 1.0
xtrue[36:50, 20:32] = 0.7
xtrue[34:52, 40:50] = 0.5
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:ny, :nx]
xtrue[(yy - 24) ** 2 + (xx - 46) ** 2 < 36] = 0.8

t = np.arange(-10, 11)
g = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 1.5) ** 2)
h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)

# Plug-and-play ADMM: the median filter replaces the proximal operator of the prior
rho = 0.1      # ADMM penalty parameter
niter = 30     # PnP-ADMM outer iterations
ncg = 20       # CG iterations for each x-update

Iden = pylops.Identity(ny * nx)
normal = Op.H @ Op + rho * Iden   # x-update system: (A^H A + rho I) x = A^H y + rho (z - u)
aty = Op.H @ y

x = np.zeros(ny * nx)
z = np.zeros(ny * nx)
u = np.zeros(ny * nx)
for _ in range(niter):
    # x-update: data fit + quadratic coupling to the denoised variable
    x = cg(normal, aty + rho * (z - u), x0=x, niter=ncg)[0]
    # z-update: 3x3 median filter as the denoiser prior
    z = median_filter((x + u).reshape(ny, nx), size=3, mode="reflect").ravel()
    # dual update
    u = u + x - z

xinv = z.reshape(ny, nx)
print(f"SNR_DB={pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Judge votes✗ ✗ ✗
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` in the current folder and ran it. It prints `SNR_DB=18.24464009665856` as its last line.

**What I chose**

- **Method:** plug-and-play ADMM. The data term is solved exactly by pylops, and the prior step is a 3×3 median filter (`scipy.ndimage.median_filter`, reflect boundaries).
- **Each iteration:**
  - x-update: minimize `0.5||Op x − y||² + ρ/2 ||x − (z − u)||²` with 20 warm-started CGLS iterations, using `pylops.VStack([Op, √ρ·I])`.
  - z-update: `z = median3x3(x + u)`.
  - dual update: `u += x − z`.
- **Output:** `z`, the denoised estimate, because it is the one that satisfies the prior.
- **Parameters:** ρ = 0.5 and 40 ADMM iterations. I picked ρ = 0.5 before running anything; it was not tuned.

**How well it worked**

| Method | SNR (dB) |
|---|---|
| Damped least squares, no prior (baseline in the script) | 16.55 |
| PnP-ADMM with 3×3 median prior, ρ = 0.5 | 18.24 |

A quick sweep over ρ in scratch copies, scored against the ground truth, showed the result is sensitive to ρ:

| ρ | 0.05 | 0.2 | 0.5 | 1.0 | 2.0 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| SNR (dB) | 20.16 | 19.44 | 18.24 | 17.42 | 16.68 |

Smaller ρ did better on this one image. Because that sweep used the ground truth, ρ = 0.05 would be an optimistic choice, so I left the script at 0.5. On real data you would need a different way to pick ρ, such as a discrepancy principle on the known noise level.

The gain over the baseline is real but modest, and it comes from the median prior being applied inside the iteration. The sweep also shows the result is not monotone in the strength of the prior, so tune ρ before relying on the number.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import cgls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr
from scipy.ndimage import median_filter

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))
xtrue[8:56, 8:56] = 0.3
xtrue[16:30, 14:40] = 1.0
xtrue[36:50, 20:32] = 0.7
xtrue[34:52, 40:50] = 0.5
yy, xx = np.mgrid[:ny, :nx]
xtrue[(yy - 24) ** 2 + (xx - 46) ** 2 < 36] = 0.8

t = np.arange(-10, 11)
g = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 1.5) ** 2)
h = np.outer(g, g)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve2D(dims=(ny, nx), h=h, offset=(10, 10))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)

# Baseline for reference: plain damped least squares (no prior)
xls = cgls(Op, y, niter=100, damp=0.05)[0].reshape(ny, nx)
print(f"BASELINE_DAMPED_LS_SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xls):.2f}")

# Plug-and-play ADMM: data term solved exactly by pylops, prior step is a
# 3x3 median filter applied to the current estimate (z-update).
rho = 0.5          # quadratic penalty weight coupling x and z
niter = 40         # ADMM iterations
cg_iter = 20       # inner CGLS iterations for the x-update

n = ny * nx
Aug = pylops.VStack([Op, np.sqrt(rho) * pylops.Identity(n)])

z = np.zeros(n)
u = np.zeros(n)
x = np.zeros(n)
for _ in range(niter):
    # x-update: min_x 0.5||Op x - y||^2 + rho/2 ||x - (z - u)||^2
    b = np.concatenate([y, np.sqrt(rho) * (z - u)])
    x = cgls(Aug, b, x0=x, niter=cg_iter, tol=1e-10)[0]
    # z-update: denoise with the 3x3 median filter
    z = median_filter((x + u).reshape(ny, nx), size=3, mode="reflect").ravel()
    # dual update
    u = u + x - z

# z is the prior-consistent (denoised) estimate, so it is the output
xinv = z.reshape(ny, nx)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I'm deblurring small blocky images (flat regions with sharp edges). A 3×3 median filter is a very good denoiser for this kind of image, so I'd like to use it as the prior inside the inversion itself, not just to clean up the result afterwards. Here is my synthetic test:

import numpy as np
import pylops

ny = nx = 64
xtrue = np.zeros((ny, nx))

y = Op @ xtrue.ravel() + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(ny * nx)

You are shown the script invert.py. Trace the data flow of the true model (xtrue, x1/x2, or Xtrue, whichever the script uses as ground truth) through it.

Claim: no value computed from the true model flows into the computation of the inverted model — i.e. it is not passed (directly or via a derived variable) into the solver call, the forward operator, the regulariser or its weight, the initial guess, data/model weights, or a support/mask/rank computed from the true model.

Stack the second-derivative operator under your convolution and solve the augmented least-squares problem with `lsqr`. I ran this on your setup, and the weight of about 3 to 10 works well here.

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Second-derivative smoothing regulariser
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n)
eps = 5.0  # regularisation weight, see below

Vop = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * D2])
yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(D2.shape[0])])  # zeros pad the penalty rows
xinv = lsqr(Vop, yaug, niter=300)[0]
```

The key points are:

- **Use the operator's real row count for the padding.** `SecondDerivative(n)` has `n` rows here, not `n-2`. Pad with `D2.shape[0]` zeros rather than hard-coding the length.
- **The weight is relative to the scale of `Op`.** Your `h` is normalised, so `Op` has gain at most 1. If you change the kernel's scale, the good `eps` changes too.

**Choosing the weight.** I swept `eps` from 1e-3 to 100 and measured the data residual `||Op x - y||` and the error `||x - xtrue||`. In a real problem you won't have `xtrue`, so the residual is what you use to choose:

| eps | residual | error vs xtrue |
|---|---|---|
| 0.001 | 0.75 | 109.0 |
| 0.1 | 0.76 | 2.80 |
| 1 | 0.77 | 0.79 |
| 3.16 | 0.78 | 0.32 |
| 10 | 0.80 | 0.36 |
| 31.6 | 1.16 | 1.38 |

- **Discrepancy principle.** Your noise is 0.05 per sample, so the residual should be about `sqrt(n) * 0.05 ≈ 0.87`. That gives `eps` of roughly 10 to 20 here.
- **L-curve.** Plot the residual against the roughness `||D2 x||` over a log-spaced sweep of `eps` and take the corner. This works when you don't know the noise level.
- **Practical rule.** Pick `eps` where the residual is just above the noise floor, and check that the result looks smooth without flattening the peaks.

I ran the sweep in a scratch script in `$TMPDIR`. Nothing in your repo was changed.
