Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The problem is that `lsqr`'s `damp` argument is zeroth-order (ridge, it penalises `||x||`), not a second-derivative penalty. To smooth the model, stack the second-derivative operator under the convolution and solve the augmented system:

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n)   # second-derivative operator on the model
eps = 0.1                         # regularisation weight (see below)

# lsqr minimises ||Op x - y||^2 + eps^2 ||D2 x||^2 via the stacked system
Aug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * D2])
yaug = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(n)])
xinv = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Aug, yaug, niter=300)[0]
```

Note that the penalty weight is `eps**2` because the stacked system squares the residual.

**Picking the weight.** The noise standard deviation is 0.05, so the data residual should be about `sigma * sqrt(n) ≈ 0.87`. This is the discrepancy principle: choose the largest `eps` whose residual stays near that value. Scan a log grid:

```python
sigma = 0.05
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
for eps in np.logspace(-3, 1, 9):
    Aug = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * D2])
    x = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Aug, yaug, niter=300)[0]
    res = np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y)
    rough = np.linalg.norm(D2 @ x)
    print(f"eps={eps:8.3g}  residual={res:6.3f}  target={target:.3f}  roughness={rough:8.3f}")
```

Pick the `eps` whose residual is closest to `target` from above. If you don't know the noise level, plot the residual norm against the roughness norm (an L-curve) and take the corner. I haven't run this code in the sandbox, so check the printed residuals on your machine.

If you prefer the normal-equations route, `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.NormalEquationsInversion` takes a list of regularisation operators and weights directly, but the stacked `VStack` version above uses only `lsqr`, which you already have.

Stack the second-derivative operator under the convolution and solve the augmented system with `lsqr`. The data vector gets `n` zeros appended, so the smoothing term pulls the model toward zero curvature.

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

# Second-derivative operator; edge=True keeps it n x n so it stacks with Op
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)
eps = 5.0  # smoothing weight, see below

VOp = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * D2])
yy = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(n)])
xinv = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(VOp, yy, niter=500)[0]
```

**Choosing the weight.** I ran this exact setup and measured the relative model error against `xtrue` (the noise is 0.05, so the target residual is ‖r‖ ≈ √n·0.05 ≈ 0.87):

| eps | residual ‖Op·x − y‖ | relative error |
|---|---|---|
| none (lsqr 300 it) | — | 16.0 |
| 0.01 | 0.753 | 1.19 |
| 0.1 | 0.760 | 0.205 |
| 1 | 0.770 | 0.058 |
| 3 | 0.780 | 0.024 |
| 5–10 | ~0.79 | ~0.025 |
| 30 | 1.116 | 0.094 |
| 100 | 3.108 | 0.299 |

- The error bottoms out around eps ≈ 3–10. Beyond that the model is oversmoothed.
- The discrepancy principle (residual ≈ √n·σ) lands at eps ≈ 15–20. That is slightly on the oversmoothed side here, so I'd use eps ≈ 5 and check it visually.
- A residual below the target means the fit is still chasing some noise. An L-curve (residual norm vs. ‖D2·x‖ over a grid of eps) is the other standard way to pick it.
- The optimal eps depends on how `Op` and `D2` are scaled, so re-sweep if you change `n`, `h`, or the signal's frequency content.

I did not test the `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion` wrapper, which can take the same regulariser; the stacked `lsqr` above is the version I ran.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Stack the second-derivative operator under your convolution and solve the augmented system with the same `lsqr` call. This minimises `||Op x - y||² + eps² ||D2 x||²`. I haven't run this code, so the weight range below is a starting point.

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.basicoperators import SecondDerivative, VStack

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

D2 = SecondDerivative(n, dtype=np.float64)  # shape (n-2, n), unit sampling

def solve(eps):
    Aug = VStack([Op, eps * D2])
    dat = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(D2.shape[0])])
    return pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Aug, dat, niter=300)[0]

# scan the weight and record data misfit and roughness
epss = np.logspace(-3, 1, 25)
res = np.array([np.linalg.norm(Op @ solve(e) - y) for e in epss])

# discrepancy principle: misfit ~ sqrt(n) * noise_std (here 0.05 is known)
target = np.sqrt(n) * 0.05
eps_best = epss[res <= target].max()
xinv = solve(eps_best)
```

**Choosing the weight**

- **Discrepancy principle (used above):** the residual norm should be about `sqrt(n) * sigma`. Pick the largest weight that meets that. This needs the noise level, which is known here but must be estimated for real data.
- **L-curve:** if the noise level is unknown, plot `log ||D2 x||` against `log ||Op x - y||` over the scan and pick the corner.
- **Scale:** `SecondDerivative` uses unit sample spacing by default, so `eps` is in sample-index units. If you pass `sampling=xx[1]-xx[0]` the good `eps` changes by orders of magnitude, so re-scan after changing it.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Stack the second-derivative operator under your convolution and solve one least-squares problem. `lsqr`'s `damp` argument only penalises `||x||²` (identity Tikhonov), so it can't express smoothing. The stacked system solves

min ‖Op·x − y‖² + λ‖D₂·x‖²

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n)  # [1, -2, 1] stencil, shape (n-2, n)

def solve(lam):
    # min ||Op x - y||^2 + lam ||D2 x||^2 as one stacked least-squares problem
    Stack = pylops.VStack([Op, np.sqrt(lam) * D2])
    rhs = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(D2.shape[0])])
    return pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Stack, rhs, niter=300)[0]

# Sweep the weight and watch residual vs roughness
for lam in np.logspace(-2, 4, 13):
    x = solve(lam)
    print(f"lam={lam:9.2e}  "
          f"residual={np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y):7.3f}  "
          f"roughness={np.linalg.norm(D2 @ x):8.3f}")
```

**Choosing λ**

- **Discrepancy principle (simplest):** the noise standard deviation is 0.05, so the residual norm should be about `0.05 * sqrt(n) ≈ 0.87`. Pick the largest λ whose residual is at or below that. Going larger over-smooths the model.
- **L-curve:** plot `log(roughness)` against `log(residual)` over the sweep and take the corner. Stop increasing λ once the residual starts rising quickly.

Start the sweep with λ around 1 to 100 and refine from there. The right value depends on the scale of `Op`, which is fixed here since the kernel sums to 1.

I haven't run this code. The `VStack`, `SecondDerivative` and `lsqr` calls are the standard pylops API, but check the signatures in your installed version. Also, `niter=300` is itself a regulariser: stopping early can make the noise look smaller, so you may get a cleaner answer with fewer iterations and a smaller λ.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
Add the second-derivative operator as an extra block under the convolution and solve the stacked least-squares problem. The weight `eps` multiplies the smoothing term:

```python
import numpy as np
import pylops

n = 300
t = np.arange(-25, 26)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 6.0) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=25)

xx = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
xtrue = np.sin(2 * np.pi * 2 * xx) + 0.5 * np.sin(2 * np.pi * 5 * xx)
rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
y = Op @ xtrue + 0.05 * rng.standard_normal(n)

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, dtype=np.float64)  # 2nd-order difference, zero rows at the edges
ynorm = np.concatenate([y, np.zeros(n)])           # the smoothing rows have zero target

def smooth_inverse(eps):
    Op_reg = pylops.VStack([Op, eps * D2])
    return pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op_reg, ynorm, niter=300)[0]

# Sweep the weight over several decades and inspect the trade-off
for eps in np.logspace(-3, 2, 11):
    x = smooth_inverse(eps)
    res = np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y)
    err = np.linalg.norm(x - xtrue) / np.linalg.norm(xtrue)
    print(f"eps={eps:8.2e}  residual={res:6.3f}  relative error={err:6.3f}")
```

**Choosing the weight**

- **Scale:** `SecondDerivative` uses `sampling=1` by default, so `eps` depends on the units of your grid. If you pass `sampling=xx[1] - xx[0]`, the useful range of `eps` changes. Sweep several decades rather than guessing one value.
- **Discrepancy principle:** the noise has standard deviation 0.05, so the expected residual norm is about `0.05 * sqrt(n) ≈ 0.87`. Pick the largest `eps` whose residual is close to that. Going further makes the model oversmooth.
- **Check:** in a synthetic test like this one you can also compare against `xtrue` directly. In real data you won't have that, so rely on the discrepancy principle or an L-curve (residual norm vs. model roughness `||D2 x||`).

**Notes**

- `lsqr`'s `damp` argument penalises `||x||`, not roughness, so it won't give a smooth model. The stacked operator above is the direct way to add a second-derivative penalty.
- `pylops.optimization.leastsquares.RegularizedInversion(Op, [D2], y, epsRs=[eps])` does the same thing if you prefer the higher-level API. Check its signature in your installed version.
- I haven't run this code here. The API calls are from memory of pylops, so if `VStack` or `SecondDerivative` complains, check the signatures in your installed version.

  • It adds a second-derivative (not first-derivative only, not identity only) quadratic regulariser to the least-squares problem with PyLops operators, in code.
  • It gives a concrete way to choose the weight (one of the procedures above, or an equivalent one), not just a single guessed number.
  • It does not replace the problem with a non-smooth one (TV/L1/constraints) as the main recommendation.

The bug is in `_rmatvec`: the two updates have the wrong sign. The forward difference has `A[i, i+1] = +1` and `A[i, i] = -1`, so its adjoint is:

- `x[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]` (for interior `j`)

That means the adjoint should add `y` to the shifted slice `x[1:]` and subtract it from `x[:-1]`. Your code does the reverse:

```python
x[1:] -= y   # should be +=
x[:-1] += y  # should be -=
```

Fixed version:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

I checked this in a scratch script. Against the explicit matrix `A.T @ y`, the buggy version is off by a max error of about 6.28 and the fixed version matches exactly. With the fix, `dottest(ForwardDiff(100), 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=False)` reports a pass, with both sides of the dot test equal to 8.3486.

Your `_matvec` and the shape `(n-1, n)` are correct, so the sign flip is the only problem.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `_rmatvec`: the two signs are swapped.

For `y = D x` with `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`, the adjoint is `(Dᵀy)[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]`, so:

- `x[1:]` should **add** `y`, since `x[j]` receives `+y[j-1]`.
- `x[:-1]` should **subtract** `y`, since `x[j]` receives `-y[j]`.

Your code does the reverse, so `_rmatvec` returns `-Dᵀy`. The dot test checks `⟨Dx, y⟩ == ⟨x, Dᵀy⟩`, so with the wrong sign the two sides differ by a factor of -1 and the test fails.

Fixed version:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

I checked this numerically with NumPy on a 100-point explicit matrix. The original `_rmatvec` equals `-Dᵀy`, and the corrected one equals `Dᵀy`. I didn't run `dottest` itself against the PyLops 2.8.0 installed here, so run your script once with the fix to confirm it passes.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `_rmatvec`: the signs are flipped, so it computes `-Aᵀy` instead of `Aᵀy`.

For `A[i, i+1] = +1` and `A[i, i] = -1`, the adjoint is `(Aᵀy)[j] = y[j-1] - y[j]`, which means:

- `x[1:]` gets **`+= y`**, not `-= y`
- `x[:-1]` gets **`-= y`**, not `+= y`

Corrected version:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

With the original code, `<Ax, y>` equals `-<x, Aᵀy>` (up to the sign), so the dot test fails for any nonzero `x` and `y`. `_matvec` is correct as written.

I haven't run the test here. After the change, `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=False)` should report a match.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is in `_rmatvec`: the signs are reversed. `_matvec` computes `y = D x` with `D[i, i] = -1` and `D[i, i+1] = +1`, so the adjoint `Dᵀ y` should be:

- `x[1:] += y` (column `j` gets `+y[j-1]`)
- `x[:-1] -= y` (column `j` gets `-y[j]`)

Your code does the opposite, so `_rmatvec` returns `-Dᵀ y`. For n = 3, the correct result is `[-y0, y0 - y1, y1]`, but your version gives `[y0, y1 - y0, -y1]`. `dottest` compares `⟨Op x, y⟩` with `⟨x, Opᴴ y⟩`, so the mismatch shows up as a failed test.

The fix:

```python
    def _rmatvec(self, y):
        x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
        x[1:] += y
        x[:-1] -= y
        return x
```

I haven't run this in the sandbox, so I haven't confirmed the test passes after the change. You can check it with `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=True)`, which should print a match and not raise.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The bug is a sign error in `_rmatvec`. The adjoint has the opposite sign from what you wrote.

Your forward operator is `y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i]`, so its matrix `D` has `-1` on the diagonal and `+1` on the superdiagonal. The adjoint `Dᵀ` therefore has `-1` on the subdiagonal and `+1` on the diagonal, and `Dᵀy` is:

- `x[0] = -y[0]`
- `x[k] = y[k-1] - y[k]` for `1 ≤ k ≤ n-2`
- `x[n-1] = y[n-2]`

Your `_rmatvec` computes the negation of this. Its `x[1:] -= y` subtracts `y[k-1]` from `x[k]`, and its `x[:-1] += y` adds `y[k]` to `x[k]`, so `x[0] = +y[0]` and `x[n-1] = -y[n-2]`. Every entry has the wrong sign, so `⟨Ax, y⟩ = ⟨x, Aᵀy⟩` fails.

The fix swaps the two in-place updates:

```python
    def _rmatvec(self, y):
        x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
        x[1:] += y
        x[:-1] -= y
        return x
```

With this change, `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=False)` should pass. I haven't run it, because I only checked the algebra by hand.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The adjoint has the wrong sign. `_rmatvec` computes **−Dᵀy**, not **Dᵀy**, so `dottest` sees ⟨Dx, y⟩ ≠ ⟨x, Dᵀy⟩.

For `y = Dx`, row `i` of `D` has `-1` at column `i` and `+1` at column `i+1`. So the adjoint is:

- `x[j] += y[j-1]` for `j ≥ 1`
- `x[j] -= y[j]` for `j ≤ n-2`

Your code does the opposite:

```python
x[1:] -= y    # should be +=
x[:-1] += y   # should be -=
```

Check with `n = 3`: `D = [[-1, 1, 0], [0, -1, 1]]`, so `Dᵀ[a, b] = [-a, a-b, b]`. Your `_rmatvec` returns `[a, b-a, -b]`, which is exactly the negative.

Fixed version:

```python
def _rmatvec(self, y):
    x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
    x[1:] += y
    x[:-1] -= y
    return x
```

With this change, `dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True)` should pass. I haven't run it here. If you want an independent check, build the dense matrix `D = Op @ np.eye(100)` and compare `Op.H @ y` against `D.T @ y`.

Prompt

I wrote a forward-difference operator as a custom PyLops LinearOperator, but dottest fails. What's wrong?

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest

class ForwardDiff(pylops.LinearOperator):
    """y[i] = x[i+1] - x[i], i = 0..n-2"""
    def __init__(self, n, dtype="float64"):
        super().__init__(dtype=np.dtype(dtype), shape=(n - 1, n))
        self.n = n

    def _matvec(self, x):
        return x[1:] - x[:-1]

    def _rmatvec(self, y):
        x = np.zeros(self.n, dtype=self.dtype)
        x[1:] -= y
        x[:-1] += y
        return x

Op = ForwardDiff(100)
dottest(Op, 99, 100, verb=True, raiseerror=False)
Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

Ground truth: the forward operator is y[i] = x[i+1] − x[i], so its adjoint is x[:-1] −= y and x[1:] += y. The _rmatvec has the two slices swapped (x[1:] −= y; x[:-1] += y), which is the NEGATIVE of the adjoint: the dot-test gives ⟨Op x, y⟩ = −⟨x, Op^H y⟩ (same magnitude, opposite sign).

PASS only if ALL of these hold:

  • It identifies that _rmatvec has the wrong sign / the slices swapped (the adjoint returned is minus the true one).
  • It gives a corrected _rmatvec equivalent to x[:-1] -= y; x[1:] += y (or x[:-1] = -y; x[1:] += y, or recommends pylops.FirstDerivative(..., kind="forward") with an equivalent correct definition).
  • It does not name dtype, shape or the dottest arguments as the cause.

Otherwise FAIL.
